### Foreign relations

Main article: [Foreign relations of Bulgaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Bulgaria)



Diplomatic missions of Bulgaria

Bulgaria became a member of the [United Nations](31769.md) in 1955. Since 1966, it has been a non-permanent member of the [Security Council](31956.md) three times, most recently from 2002 to 2003.[^cite_note-249] It was also among the founding nations of the [Organisation for Security and Co-operation in Europe](22796.md) (OSCE) in 1975. Euro-Atlantic integration has been a priority since the fall of communism, although the communist leadership also had aspirations of leaving the [Warsaw Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw_Pact) and joining the [European Communities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Communities) by 1987.[^cite_note-250][^cite_note-251] Bulgaria signed the European Union [Treaty of Accession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Accession_2005) on 25 April 2005,[^cite_note-ec-252] and became a full member of the European Union on 1 January 2007.[^cite_note-Ind-135] In addition, it has a tripartite economic and diplomatic collaboration with Romania and Greece,[^cite_note-253] good ties with [China](5405.md)[^cite_note-254] and [Vietnam](202354.md)[^cite_note-255] and a historical relationship with Russia.[^cite_note-256]

Bulgaria deployed significant numbers of both civilian and military advisors in Soviet-allied countries like [Nicaragua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicaraguan_Revolution)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEArms_Sales-257] and [Libya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Socialist_People's_Libyan_Arab_Jamahiriya) during the [Cold War](325329.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEForeign_Affairs_in_the_1960s_and_1970s-258] The first deployment of foreign troops on Bulgarian soil since World War II occurred in 2001, when the country hosted six [KC-135 Stratotanker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KC-135_Stratotanker) aircraft and 200 support personnel for the war effort in Afghanistan.[^cite_note-CENTCOM-28] International military relations were further expanded with accession to [NATO](21133.md) in March 2004[^cite_note-nato-134] and the US-Bulgarian Defence Cooperation Agreement signed in April 2006. [Bezmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bezmer_Air_Base) and [Graf Ignatievo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graf_Ignatievo_Air_Base) air bases, the [Novo Selo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novo_Selo_Range) training range, and a logistics centre in [Aytos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aytos_Logistics_Center) subsequently became [joint military training facilities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_joint_US-Bulgarian_military_bases) cooperatively used by the United States and Bulgarian militaries.[^cite_note-State-259][^cite_note-260] Despite its active international defence collaborations, Bulgaria ranks as among the most peaceful countries globally, tying 6th alongside Iceland regarding domestic and international conflicts, and 26th on average in the [Global Peace Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Peace_Index).[^cite_note-261]

Following the [2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md), Bulgaria assisted Ukraine;[^cite_note-262] in 2023, after Gazprom illegally stopped exporting gas to Bulgaria, the country in turn stopped importing Russian oil and gas.[^cite_note-263]

### Military

Main article: [Bulgarian Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgarian_Armed_Forces)

Bulgarian MiG-29 fighters in flight

[Mikoyan MiG-29](185653.md) jet fighters of the [Bulgarian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgarian_Air_Force)

The [Bulgarian Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgarian_Armed_Forces) are the military of Bulgaria and are composed of [land forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgarian_land_forces), [navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgarian_Navy) and an [air force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgarian_Air_Force). The Armed Forces have 36,950 active troops, supplemented by 3,000 [reservists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reservist).[^cite_note-264] The land forces consist of two [mechanised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mechanized_infantry) [brigades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brigade) and eight independent [regiments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regiment) and [battalions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battalion); the air force operates 106 aircraft and [air defence](146640.md) systems across six air bases, and the navy operates various ships, helicopters and coastal defence weapons.[^cite_note-AF_plan-265] Military inventory mainly consists of Soviet equipment like [Mikoyan MiG-29](185653.md) and [Sukhoi Su-25](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukhoi_Su-25) jets,[^cite_note-266] [S-300PT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S-300_missile_system) air defence systems[^cite_note-267] and [SS-21 Scarab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SS-21_Scarab) short-range [ballistic missiles](38025.md).[^cite_note-268] The Armed Forces are modernising with [F-16 Block 70](11642.md) fighter jets, new multi-purpose corvettes and other modern NATO-standard equipment. Bulgaria is in the process of buying new US-built [Stryker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stryker) vehicles, new [155 mm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/155_mm_caliber) [self-propelled howitzers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-propelled_artillery), new [3D early-warning radars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early-warning_radar), new [surface-to-air missiles](182664.md) and more.[^cite_note-269]

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Bulgaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Bulgaria)

Graph showing GDP and unemployment

Economic growth (green) and unemployment (blue) statistics since 2001

Bulgaria has an [advanced](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advanced_economy),[^cite_note-IMF_classification-270] [high-income economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-income_economy) where the private sector accounts for more than 70% of [GDP](12594.md).[^cite_note-271][^cite_note-272] From a largely agricultural country with a predominantly rural population in 1948, by the 1980s Bulgaria had transformed into an industrial economy, with scientific and technological research at the top of its budgetary expenditure priorities.[^cite_note-273] The loss of [COMECON](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COMECON) markets in 1990 and the subsequent "[shock therapy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shock_therapy_\(economics\))" of the [planned system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planned_economy) caused a steep decline in industrial and agricultural production, ultimately followed by an economic collapse in 1997.[^cite_note-Economies-274][^cite_note-275] The economy largely recovered during a period of rapid growth several years later,[^cite_note-Economies-274] but the average salary of 2,072 leva (\$1,142) per month remains the lowest in the EU.[^cite_note-276]

A [balanced budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balanced_budget) was achieved in 2003 and the country began running a [surplus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Budget_surplus) the following year.[^cite_note-OECD1-277] Expenditures amounted to \$21.15 billion and revenues were \$21.67 billion in 2017.[^cite_note-278] Most government spending on institutions is earmarked for security. The ministries of defence, the interior and justice are allocated the largest share of the annual government budget, whereas those responsible for the environment, tourism and energy receive the least funding.[^cite_note-2018budget-279] Taxes form the bulk of government revenue[^cite_note-2018budget-279] at 30% of GDP.[^cite_note-280] Bulgaria has some of the lowest corporate income [tax rates in the EU](11015873.md) at a flat 10% rate.[^cite_note-281] The tax system is two-tier. [Value added tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Value_added_tax), [excise duties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Excise_duties), corporate and personal income tax are national, whereas real estate, inheritance, and vehicle taxes are levied by local authorities.[^cite_note-282] Strong economic performance in the early 2000s reduced [government debt](695460.md) from 79.6% in 1998 to 14.1% in 2008.[^cite_note-OECD1-277] It has since increased to 22.6% of GDP by 2022, but remains the second lowest in the EU.[^cite_note-283]



The [Yugozapaden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yugozapaden) [planning area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First-level_NUTS_of_the_European_Union) is Bulgaria's most developed region.

The [Yugozapaden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yugozapaden) [planning area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First-level_NUTS_of_the_European_Union) is the most developed region with a [per capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Per_capita) gross domestic product ([PPP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purchasing_power_parity)) of \$29,816 in 2018.[^cite_note-284] It includes the capital city and the surrounding [Sofia Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sofia_Province), which alone generate 42% of national gross domestic product despite hosting only 22% of the population.[^cite_note-285][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENSI_Census_data2017-286] GDP per capita (in [PPS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purchasing_power_standard)) and the cost of living in 2019 stood at 53 and 52.8% of the EU average (100%), respectively.[^cite_note-287][^cite_note-288] National PPP GDP was estimated at \$143.1 billion in 2016, with a per capita value of \$20,116.[^cite_note-imf2-289] Economic growth statistics take into account illegal transactions from the [informal economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Informal_economy), which is the largest in the EU as a percentage of economic output.[^cite_note-290][^cite_note-291]

After several consecutive years of high growth, repercussions of the [2008 financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_financial_crisis) resulted in a 3.6% contraction of GDP in 2009 and increased unemployment.[^cite_note-292][^cite_note-293] Positive growth was restored in 2010 but intercompany debt exceeded \$59 billion, meaning that 60% of all Bulgarian companies were mutually indebted.[^cite_note-294] By 2012, it had increased to \$97 billion, or 227% of GDP.[^cite_note-295] The government implemented strict austerity measures with IMF and EU encouragement to some positive fiscal results, but the social consequences of these measures, such as increased [income inequality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_inequality) and accelerated outward migration, have been "catastrophic" according to the [International Trade Union Confederation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Trade_Union_Confederation).[^cite_note-296]

Siphoning of public funds to the families and relatives of politicians from incumbent parties has resulted in fiscal and welfare losses to society.[^cite_note-297][^cite_note-298] Bulgaria ranked 71st in the [Corruption Perceptions Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_Perceptions_Index) in 2017[^cite_note-299] and experiences the worst levels of [corruption](3608404.md) in the European Union, a phenomenon that remains a source of profound public discontent.[^cite_note-cloud-300][^cite_note-301] Along with organised crime, corruption has resulted in a rejection of the country's [Schengen Area](16565139.md) application and withdrawal of foreign investment,[^cite_note-302][^cite_note-303][^cite_note-reuters_USKBN1F61EQ-304] though the country [officially became a full member of the zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accession_of_Bulgaria_and_Romania_to_the_Schengen_Area) in January 2025.[^cite_note-305] Government officials reportedly engage in embezzlement, influence trading, government procurement violations and bribery with impunity.[^cite_note-SG1-306] Government procurement in particular is a critical area in corruption risk. An estimated 10 billion leva (\$5.99 billion) of state budget and [European cohesion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Structural_Funds_and_Cohesion_Fund) funds are spent on public tenders each year;[^cite_note-307] nearly 14 billion (\$8.38 billion) were spent on public contracts in 2017 alone.[^cite_note-308] A large share of these contracts are awarded to a few politically connected[^cite_note-309] companies amid widespread irregularities, procedure violations and tailor-made award criteria.[^cite_note-310] Despite repeated criticism from the [European Commission](9974.md),[^cite_note-reuters_USKBN1F61EQ-304] EU institutions refrain from taking measures against Bulgaria because it supports Brussels on a number of issues, unlike [Poland](22936.md) or [Hungary](13275.md).[^cite_note-cloud-300]

### Currency

See also: [Bulgarian lev](268071.md) and [Bulgaria and the euro](5516984.md)

From 1880 to 2026, the [Bulgarian National Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgarian_National_Bank) issued its own national currency, [lev](268071.md), which was pegged to the euro at a rate of 1.95583 leva per euro.[^cite_note-311] When Bulgaria [joined the EU in 2007](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_enlargement_of_the_European_Union) it committed to join the [eurozone](184391.md) and replace its currency, the lev, with the [euro](9472.md). In February 2025, the country officially requested off-cycle assessments of their convergence by the [European Commission](9974.md) and [ECB](9474.md) to determine the country's readiness. The 2025 convergence reports published on 4 June 2025 concluded that Bulgaria met the [convergence criteria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convergence_criteria). On 8 July 2025, the [European Parliament](9581.md) endorsed Bulgaria's entry in the eurozone and the [Council of the European Union](5866.md) adopted the final three legislative acts required for the admission.

Bulgaria adopted the euro as currency, becoming a part of the [euro area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euro_area) on 1 January 2026.[^cite_note-312] The lev and euro were in dual circulation until 31 January 2026; after 1 February 2026, the euro has been the nation's sole currency.[^cite_note-313]

### Structure and sectors

A view of Rila mountain

[Rila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rila), the highest mountain in Southeast Europe

A view of Nesebar

[Nesebar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nesebar)

The labour force is 3.36 million people,[^cite_note-314] of whom 6.8% are employed in agriculture, 26.6% in industry and 66.6% in the services sector.[^cite_note-315] Extraction of metals and minerals, production of [chemicals](58721.md), [machine building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Machinery_industry), steel, biotechnology, tobacco, food processing and [petroleum refining](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refined_petroleum_fuel) are among the major industrial activities.[^cite_note-316][^cite_note-317][^cite_note-318] Mining alone employs 24,000 people and generates about 5% of the country's GDP; the number of employed in all mining-related industries is 120,000.[^cite_note-319][^cite_note-Ore_exports-320] Bulgaria is Europe's fifth-largest coal producer.[^cite_note-Ore_exports-320][^cite_note-321] Local deposits of coal, iron, copper and lead are vital for the manufacturing and energy sectors.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEResource_Base-322] The main destinations of Bulgarian exports outside the EU are Turkey, China and Serbia, while Russia, Turkey and China are by far the largest import partners. Most of the exports are manufactured goods, machinery, chemicals, fuel products and food.[^cite_note-323] Two-thirds of food and agricultural exports go to [OECD](33853117.md) countries.[^cite_note-324]

Although cereal and vegetable output dropped by 40% between 1990 and 2008,[^cite_note-325] output in grains has since increased, and the 2016–2017 season registered the biggest grain output in a decade.[^cite_note-UNdata-326][^cite_note-327] [Maize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maize), [barley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barley), [oats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oats) and [rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rice) are also grown. Quality [Oriental tobacco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_tobacco) is a significant industrial crop.[^cite_note-328] Bulgaria is also the largest producer globally of [lavender](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lavender_oil) and [rose oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rose_oil), both widely used in fragrances.[^cite_note-CENTCOM-28][^cite_note-329][^cite_note-330][^cite_note-331] Within the services sector, [tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Bulgaria) is a significant contributor to economic growth. [Sofia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sofia), [Plovdiv](182553.md), [Veliko Tarnovo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Veliko_Tarnovo), coastal resorts [Albena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albena), [Golden Sands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Sands) and [Sunny Beach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunny_Beach) and winter resorts [Bansko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bansko), [Pamporovo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pamporovo) and [Borovets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borovets) are some of the locations most visited by tourists.[^cite_note-332][^cite_note-333] Most visitors are Romanian, Turkish, Greek and German.[^cite_note-334] Tourism is additionally encouraged through the [100 Tourist Sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/100_Tourist_Sites_of_Bulgaria) system.[^cite_note-335]

### Science and technology

Main article: [Science and technology in Bulgaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_and_technology_in_Bulgaria)

A SpaceX Falcon 9 rocket launching BulgariaSat-1 in June 2017

The launch of BulgariaSat-1 by [SpaceX](832774.md)

Spending on [research and development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Research_and_development) amounts to 0.78% of GDP,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENSI_Brochure201819-336] and the bulk of public R\&D funding goes to the [Bulgarian Academy of Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgarian_Academy_of_Sciences) (BAS).[^cite_note-EUpresidency-337] Private businesses accounted for more than 73% of R\&D expenditures and employed 42% of Bulgaria's 22,000 researchers in 2015.[^cite_note-R\&D_spending-338] The same year, Bulgaria ranked 39th out of 50 countries in the [Bloomberg Innovation Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bloomberg_Innovation_Index), the highest score being in education (24th) and the lowest in value-added manufacturing (48th).[^cite_note-339] Bulgaria was ranked 37th in the [Global Innovation Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Innovation_Index) in 2025.[^cite_note-340][^cite_note-341] Chronic government underinvestment in research since 1990 has forced many professionals in science and engineering to leave Bulgaria.[^cite_note-342]

Despite the lack of funding, research in chemistry, [materials science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Materials_science) and [physics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physics) remains strong.[^cite_note-EUpresidency-337] Antarctic research is actively carried out through the [St. Kliment Ohridski Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Kliment_Ohridski_Base) on [Livingston Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Livingston_Island) in [Western Antarctica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Antarctica).[^cite_note-343][^cite_note-344] The [information and communication technologies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_and_communication_technologies) (ICT) sector generates three per cent of economic output and employs 40,000[^cite_note-345] to 51,000 software engineers.[^cite_note-346] Bulgaria was known as a "Communist [Silicon Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silicon_Valley)" during the Soviet era due to its key role in [COMECON](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COMECON) computing technology production.[^cite_note-347] A concerted effort by the communist government to teach computing and IT skills in schools also indirectly made Bulgaria a major source of [computer viruses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computer_virus) in the 1980s and 90s.[^cite_note-348] The country is a regional leader in [high performance computing](37153.md): it operates *Avitohol*, the most powerful supercomputer in Southeast Europe, and will host one of the eight [petascale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petascale_computing) [EuroHPC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_High-Performance_Computing_Joint_Undertaking) supercomputers.[^cite_note-349][^cite_note-350]

Bulgaria has made numerous contributions to [space exploration](28431.md).[^cite_note-Interkosmos-351] These include two scientific satellites, more than 200 payloads and 300 experiments in Earth orbit, as well as [two cosmonauts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgarian_cosmonaut_program) since 1971.[^cite_note-Interkosmos-351] Bulgaria was the first country to grow [wheat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wheat) [in space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plants_in_space) with its [Svet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SVET_plant_growth_system) [greenhouses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenhouse) on the [Mir space station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mir_space_station).[^cite_note-352][^cite_note-353] It was involved in the development of the [Granat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Granat) [gamma-ray observatory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamma-ray_astronomy)[^cite_note-RESS-354] and the [Vega programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vega_program), particularly in modelling trajectories and guidance [algorithms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algorithms) for both Vega probes.[^cite_note-355][^cite_note-356] Bulgarian instruments have been used in the [exploration of Mars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exploration_of_Mars), including a spectrometer that took the first high quality [spectroscopic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spectroscopy) images of Martian moon [Phobos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phobos_\(moon\)) with the [Phobos 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phobos_2) probe.[^cite_note-Interkosmos-351][^cite_note-RESS-354] [Cosmic radiation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosmic_ray) en route to and around the planet has been mapped by [Liulin-ML](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liulin_type_instruments) dosimeters on the [ExoMars TGO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ExoMars_Trace_Gas_Orbiter).[^cite_note-357] [Variants of these instruments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RADOM-7) have also been fitted on the [International Space Station](15043.md) and the [Chandrayaan-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chandrayaan-1) lunar probe.[^cite_note-358][^cite_note-359] Another lunar mission, [SpaceIL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceIL)'s *Beresheet*, was also equipped with a Bulgarian-manufactured imaging payload.[^cite_note-360] Bulgaria's first [geostationary communications satellite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geosynchronous_satellite)—[BulgariaSat-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BulgariaSat-1)—was launched by [SpaceX](832774.md) in 2017.[^cite_note-361]

### Infrastructure

Main articles: [Energy in Bulgaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_in_Bulgaria) and [Transport in Bulgaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Bulgaria)

Telephone services are widely available, and a central digital trunk line connects most regions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELibrary_of_Congress200614-362] [Vivacom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vivacom) (BTC) serves more than 90% of fixed lines and is one of the three operators providing mobile services, along with [A1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mtel_\(Bulgaria\)) and [Yettel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telenor_\(Bulgaria\)).[^cite_note-363][^cite_note-364] [Internet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet) penetration stood at 69.2% of the population aged 16–74 and 78.9% of households in 2020.[^cite_note-365][^cite_note-366]

Bulgaria's strategic geographic location and well-developed energy sector make it a key European energy centre despite its lack of significant fossil fuel deposits.[^cite_note-367] Thermal power plants generate 48.9% of electricity, followed by [nuclear power](22153.md) from the [Kozloduy reactors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kozloduy_Nuclear_Power_Plant) (34.8%) and [renewable sources](25784.md) (16.3%).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENSI_Brochure201847-368] Equipment for a second nuclear power station at [Belene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belene_Nuclear_Power_Plant) has been acquired, but the fate of the project remains uncertain.[^cite_note-369] Installed capacity amounts to 12,668 MW, allowing Bulgaria to exceed domestic demand and export energy.[^cite_note-370]

The national road network has a total length of 19,512 kilometres (12,124 mi),[^cite_note-371] of which 19,235 kilometres (11,952 mi) are paved. Railroads are a major mode of freight transportation, although highways carry a progressively larger share of freight. Bulgaria has 6,238 kilometres (3,876 mi) of railway track,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELibrary_of_Congress200614-362] with rail links available to Romania, Turkey, Greece, and Serbia, and express trains serving direct routes to [Kyiv](585629.md), [Minsk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minsk), [Moscow](19004.md) and [Saint Petersburg](24320051.md).[^cite_note-372] Sofia is the country's air travel hub, while Varna and Burgas are the principal maritime trade ports.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELibrary_of_Congress200614-362]

## Demographics

Main article: [Demographics of Bulgaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Bulgaria)

Ethnic groups in Bulgaria (2021 census)

[^cite_note-Infostat-373]

[^cite_note-NSI2021-374]

1. [Bulgarians](151876.md) (84.6%)

2. [Turks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgarian_Turks) (8.40%)

3. [Romani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romani_people_in_Bulgaria) (4.41%)

4. Others (1.31%)

5. Undeclared (1.31%)

According to the government's official 2022 estimate, the population of Bulgaria consists of 6,447,710 people, down from 6,519,789 according to the last official census in 2021.[^cite_note-375][^cite_note-NSI2021-374] The majority of the population, 72.5%, reside in urban areas.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENSI_Census_data20113-376] As of 2019, Sofia is the most populated urban centre with 1,241,675 people, followed by [Plovdiv](182553.md) (346,893), [Varna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Varna,_Bulgaria) (336,505), [Burgas](86758.md) (202,434) and [Ruse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruse,_Bulgaria) (142,902).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENSI_Census_data2017-286] [Bulgarians](151876.md) are the main ethnic group and constitute 84.6% of the population. [Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turks_in_Bulgaria) and [Roma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roma_in_Bulgaria) minorities account for 8.4 and 4.4%, respectively; some 40 smaller minorities account for 1.3%, and 1.3% do not self-identify with an ethnic group.[^cite_note-Infostat-373][^cite_note-NSI2021-374] The Roma minority is usually underestimated in census data and may represent up to 11% of the population.[^cite_note-377][^cite_note-378] Population density is 55–60 per square kilometre (ultimo 2023), almost half the European Union average.[^cite_note-379]

Bulgaria is in a state of demographic crisis.[^cite_note-380][^cite_note-BNR_crisis-381] It has had negative population growth since 1989, when the post-Cold War economic collapse caused a long-lasting [emigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emigration) wave.[^cite_note-DW_Entry-382] Some 937,000 to 1,200,000 people—mostly young adults—had left the country by 2005.[^cite_note-DW_Entry-382][^cite_note-383] The majority of children are born to unmarried women.[^cite_note-384] In 2024, the average [total fertility rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_fertility_rate) (TFR) in Bulgaria was 1.59 children per woman,[^cite_note-385] a slight increase from 1.56 in 2018,[^cite_note-386] and well above the all-time low of 1.1 in 1997, but still below the replacement rate of 2.1 and considerably below the historical high of 5.83 children per woman in 1905.[^cite_note-387] Bulgaria thus has one of the oldest populations in the world, with an average age of 43 years.[^cite_note-388] Furthermore, a third of all households consist of only one person and 75.5% of families do not have children under the age of 16.[^cite_note-BNR_crisis-381] The resulting birth rates are among the lowest in the world[^cite_note-389][^cite_note-390] while [death rates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_rate) are among the highest.[^cite_note-391]

Bulgaria scores high in [gender equality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_equality), ranking 18th in the 2018 [Global Gender Gap Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Gender_Gap_Report).[^cite_note-WEF-392] Although [women's suffrage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_suffrage) was enabled relatively late, in 1937, women today have equal political rights, high workforce participation and legally mandated [equal pay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equal_pay_for_equal_work).[^cite_note-WEF-392] In 2021, market research agency *Reboot Online* ranked Bulgaria as the best European country for women to work.[^cite_note-393] Bulgaria has the highest ratio of female [ICT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_and_communications_technology) researchers in the EU,[^cite_note-394] as well as the second-highest ratio of females in the technology sector at 44.6% of the workforce. High levels of female participation are a [legacy of the Socialist era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_roles_in_post-communist_Central_and_Eastern_Europe).[^cite_note-395]

### Health

Main article: [Health in Bulgaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_in_Bulgaria)

High death rates result from a combination of an ageing population, high numbers of people at risk of poverty, and a weak [healthcare system](60976.md).[^cite_note-397] Over 80% of deaths are due to [cancer](105219.md) and [cardiovascular conditions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardiovascular_conditions); nearly a fifth of those are avoidable.[^cite_note-398] Although [healthcare in Bulgaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_Bulgaria) is nominally universal,[^cite_note-Health_system-399] [out-of-pocket expenses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Out-of-pocket_expenses) account for nearly half of all healthcare spending, significantly limiting access to medical care.[^cite_note-400] Other problems disrupting care provision are the emigration of doctors due to low wages, understaffed and under-equipped regional hospitals, supply shortages and frequent changes to the basic service package for those insured.[^cite_note-401][^cite_note-402] The 2018 Bloomberg Health Care Efficiency Index ranked Bulgaria last out of 56 countries.[^cite_note-403] Average [life expectancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_expectancy) is 74.8 years, compared with an EU average of 80.99 and a world average of 72.38.[^cite_note-404][^cite_note-405]

### Education

Main article: [Education in Bulgaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Bulgaria)

Sofia University building

The Rectorate of Sofia University

Public expenditures for education are far below the European Union average as well.[^cite_note-UNICEF-406] Educational standards [were once high](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Bulgaria),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELibrary_of_Congress20066-407] but have declined significantly since the early 2000s.[^cite_note-UNICEF-406] Bulgarian students were among the highest-scoring in the world in terms of reading in 2001, performing better than their Canadian and German counterparts; by 2006, scores in reading, mathematics and science had dropped. By 2018, [Programme for International Student Assessment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Programme_for_International_Student_Assessment) studies found 47% of pupils in the 9th grade to be [functionally illiterate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Functional_illiteracy) in reading and natural sciences.[^cite_note-408] Average basic [literacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literacy) stands high at 98.4% with no significant difference between sexes.[^cite_note-409] The [Ministry of Education and Science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Education_and_Science_\(Bulgaria\)) partially funds public schools, colleges and universities, sets criteria for textbooks and oversees the publishing process. Education in primary and secondary public schools is free and compulsory.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELibrary_of_Congress20066-407] The process spans 12 grades, in which grades one through eight are primary and nine through twelve are secondary level. Higher education consists of a 4-year [bachelor](188874.md) degree and a 1-year [master's](170346.md) degree.[^cite_note-410] Bulgaria's highest-ranked higher education institution is [Sofia University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sofia_University).[^cite_note-411][^cite_note-412]

### Language

Main article: [Languages of Bulgaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Bulgaria)

[Bulgarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgarian_language) is the only language with official status.[^cite_note-413] It belongs to the [Slavic group of languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavic_group_of_languages) but has a number of grammatical peculiarities that set it apart from other Slavic languages: these include a complex verbal morphology (which also codes for distinctions in [evidentiality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evidentiality)), the absence of [noun cases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noun_cases) and [infinitives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infinitives), and the use of a suffixed [definite article](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Definite_article).[^cite_note-414]

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Bulgaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Bulgaria)



[St. Alexander Nevsky Cathedral, Sofia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Nevsky_Cathedral,_Sofia)

Bulgaria is a [secular state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secular_state) with guaranteed [freedom of religion](46006.md) by constitution, but [Eastern Orthodox Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Christianity) is designated as the traditional religion of the country.[^cite_note-415] Approximately two-thirds of Bulgarians identify as Eastern Orthodox Christians.[^cite_note-NSI2021-374] The [Bulgarian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgarian_Orthodox_Church) was the first church apart from the [Four Ancient Patriarchates of the Eastern Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organization_of_the_Eastern_Orthodox_Church#Four_ancient_patriarchates)—in [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecumenical_Patriarchate_of_Constantinople), [Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Patriarchate_of_Alexandria), [Antioch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Patriarchate_of_Antioch) and [Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Patriarchate_of_Jerusalem)—and the first national church to gain [autocephalous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autocephalous) status in 927 AD.[^cite_note-416][^cite_note-417] The Bulgarian Patriarchate has 12 [dioceses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diocese) and over 2,000 priests.[^cite_note-418]

[Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Bulgaria) are the second-largest religious community and constitute approximately 10% of Bulgaria's population. A 2011 survey of 850 Muslims in Bulgaria found 30% professing to be "deeply religious" and 50% as just "religious". According to the study, some religious teachings, like [Islamic funeral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_funeral), have been traditionally incorporated and are widely practised while other major ones are less observed, such as the [Muslim prayer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_prayer) or abstaining from [drinking alcohol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcohol_in_Islam), [eating pork](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_dietary_laws), and [cohabitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cohabitation).[^cite_note-419]

Other important religions include [Roman Catholicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_Bulgaria) and [Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Bulgaria), whose history in Bulgaria dates back to the early [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages), the [Armenian Apostolic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_Apostolic_Church), as well as various Protestant denominations, all of which stand for around 2% of Bulgaria's population. An ever increasing number of Bulgarians are either [irreligious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion) or unaffiliated with any religion, a percentage that has been growing rapidly over the past 20 years, from 3.9% in 2001, through 9.3% in 2011 and all the way to 15.9% in 2021.[^cite_note-NSI2021-374][^cite_note-420][^cite_note-421][^cite_note-422]

According to the most recent census of 2021 the religious denominations of the population are as follows: [Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Bulgaria) (71.5%), [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Bulgaria) (10.8%), other religions (0.1%). A further 12.4% were unaffiliated or did not respond.[^cite_note-423][^cite_note-Infostat2-424][^cite_note-NSI2021-374]

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Bulgaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Bulgaria)

Bulgarian Kuker

*[Kuker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kukeri)* in [Lesichovo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lesichovo)

&#x20;Contemporary Bulgarian culture blends the formal culture that helped forge a national consciousness towards the end of Ottoman rule with millennia-old folk traditions.[^cite_note-Cultural_life-425] An essential element of Bulgarian folklore is fire, used to banish evil spirits and illnesses. Many of these are personified as witches, whereas other creatures like [zmey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavic_dragon) and [samodiva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samodiva_\(folklore\)) ([veela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supernatural_beings_in_Slavic_religion)) are either benevolent guardians or ambivalent tricksters.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMacDermott199864–70-426] Some rituals against evil spirits have survived and are still practised, most notably [kukeri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kukeri) and [survakari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Survakane).[^cite_note-427] [Martenitsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martenitsa) is also widely celebrated.[^cite_note-428] [Nestinarstvo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nestinarstvo), a ritual fire-dance of Thracian origin, is included in the list of [UNESCO Intangible Cultural Heritage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNESCO_Intangible_Cultural_Heritage_Lists).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMacDermott1998226-429][^cite_note-430] [Nine historical and natural objects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_Bulgaria) are [UNESCO World Heritage Sites](44940.md):  [Pirin National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pirin_National_Park), [Sreburna Nature Reserve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Srebarna_Nature_Reserve), the [Madara Rider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madara_Rider), the Thracian tombs in [Sveshtari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thracian_Tomb_of_Sveshtari) and [Kazanlak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thracian_Tomb_of_Kazanlak), the [Rila Monastery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rila_Monastery), the [Boyana Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boyana_Church), the [Rock-hewn Churches of Ivanovo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock-hewn_Churches_of_Ivanovo) and the ancient city of [Nesebar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nesebar).[^cite_note-431] The Rila Monastery was established by Saint [John of Rila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_of_Rila), Bulgaria's [patron saint](68055.md), whose life has been the subject of numerous literary accounts since Medieval times.[^cite_note-EBLiterature-432]

A page of a medieval manuscript

A page with the *Alphabet Prayer* by [Constantine of Preslav](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine_of_Preslav)

The establishment of the [Preslav](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preslav_Literary_School) and [Ohrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ohrid_Literary_School) literary schools in the 10th century is associated with a golden period in Bulgarian literature during the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages).[^cite_note-EBLiterature-432] The schools' emphasis on Christian [scriptures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_text) made the Bulgarian Empire a centre of Slavic culture, bringing Slavs under the influence of Christianity and providing them with [a written language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Church_Slavonic).[^cite_note-433][^cite_note-434][^cite_note-435] Its alphabet, [Cyrillic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyrillic) script, was developed by the Preslav Literary School.[^cite_note-436] The [Tarnovo Literary School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarnovo_Literary_School), on the other hand, is associated with a Silver age of literature defined by high-quality manuscripts on historical or mystical themes under the [Asen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asen_dynasty) and [Shishman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Shishman) dynasties.[^cite_note-EBLiterature-432] Many literary and artistic masterpieces were destroyed by the Ottoman conquerors, and artistic activities did not re-emerge until the [National Revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgarian_National_Revival) in the 19th century.[^cite_note-Cultural_life-425] The enormous body of work of [Ivan Vazov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_Vazov) (1850–1921) covered every genre and touched upon every facet of Bulgarian society, bridging pre-Liberation works with literature of the newly established state.[^cite_note-EBLiterature-432] Notable later works are *[Bay Ganyo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bay_Ganyo)* by [Aleko Konstantinov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleko_Konstantinov), the [Nietzschean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Nietzsche) poetry of [Pencho Slaveykov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pencho_Slaveykov), the [Symbolist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Symbolism_\(arts\)) poetry of [Peyo Yavorov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peyo_Yavorov) and [Dimcho Debelyanov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dimcho_Debelyanov), the [Marxist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marxism)-inspired works of [Geo Milev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geo_Milev) and [Nikola Vaptsarov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikola_Vaptsarov), and the [Socialist realism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Realism) novels of [Dimitar Dimov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dimitar_Dimov) and [Dimitar Talev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dimitar_Talev).[^cite_note-EBLiterature-432] [Tzvetan Todorov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tzvetan_Todorov) is a notable contemporary author,[^cite_note-437] while Bulgarian-born [Elias Canetti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elias_Canetti) was awarded the [Nobel Prize in Literature](23385442.md) in 1981.[^cite_note-438]

А religious visual arts heritage includes [frescoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fresco), [murals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mural) and [icons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Icon), many produced by the medieval [Tarnovo Artistic School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Painting_of_the_Tarnovo_Artistic_School).[^cite_note-439] Like literature, it was not until the National Revival when Bulgarian visual arts began to reemerge. [Zahari Zograf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zahari_Zograf) was a pioneer of the visual arts in the pre-Liberation era.[^cite_note-Cultural_life-425] After the Liberation, [Ivan Mrkvička](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_Mrkvi%C4%8Dka), [Anton Mitov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anton_Mitov), [Vladimir Dimitrov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Dimitrov), [Tsanko Lavrenov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsanko_Lavrenov) and [Zlatyu Boyadzhiev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zlatyu_Boyadzhiev) introduced newer styles and substance, depicting scenery from Bulgarian villages, old towns and historical subjects. [Christo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christo) is the most famous Bulgarian artist of the 21st century, known for his outdoor installations.[^cite_note-Cultural_life-425]

Folk music is by far the most extensive traditional art and has slowly developed throughout the ages as a fusion of Far Eastern, Oriental, medieval Eastern Orthodox and standard Western European tonalities and modes.[^cite_note-440] Bulgarian folk music has a distinctive sound and uses a wide range of traditional instruments, such as [gadulka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gadulka), [gaida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaida), [kaval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaval) and [tupan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Davul). A distinguishing feature is *extended rhythmical time*, which has no equivalent in the rest of European music.[^cite_note-CENTCOM-28] The [State Television Female Vocal Choir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgarian_State_Television_Female_Vocal_Choir) won a [Grammy Award](44635.md) in 1990 for its performances of Bulgarian folk music.[^cite_note-441] Written musical composition can be traced back to the works of [Yoan Kukuzel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoan_Kukuzel) (c. 1280–1360),[^cite_note-442] but modern classical music began with [Emanuil Manolov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emanuil_Manolov), who composed the first Bulgarian [opera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opera) in 1890.[^cite_note-Cultural_life-425] [Pancho Vladigerov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pancho_Vladigerov) and [Petko Staynov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petko_Staynov) further enriched [symphony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Symphony), ballet and opera, which singers [Ghena Dimitrova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghena_Dimitrova), [Boris Christoff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boris_Christoff), [Ljuba Welitsch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ljuba_Welitsch) and [Nicolai Ghiaurov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicolai_Ghiaurov) elevated to a world-class level.[^cite_note-449] Bulgarian performers have gained acclaim in other genres like [electropop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electropop) ([Mira Aroyo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mira_Aroyo)), [jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz) ([Milcho Leviev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milcho_Leviev)) and blends of jazz and folk ([Ivo Papazov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivo_Papazov)).[^cite_note-Cultural_life-425]

The [Bulgarian National Radio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgarian_National_Radio), [bTV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BTV_\(Bulgaria\)) and daily newspapers *[Trud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trud_\(Bulgarian_newspaper\))*, *[Dnevnik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dnevnik_\(Bulgaria\))* and *[24 Chasa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/24_Chasa)* are some of the largest national media outlets.[^cite_note-450] [Bulgarian media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_of_Bulgaria) were described as generally unbiased in their reporting in the early 2000s and print media had no legal restrictions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELibrary_of_Congress200618,_23-451] Since then, [freedom of the press](105323.md) has deteriorated to the point where Bulgaria scores 111th globally in the World Press Freedom Index, lower than all European Union members and membership candidate states. The government has diverted EU funds to sympathetic media outlets and bribed others to be less critical on problematic topics, while attacks against individual journalists have increased.[^cite_note-RSF-452][^cite_note-guardian1-453] Collusion between politicians, oligarchs and the media is widespread.[^cite_note-RSF-452]

[Bulgarian cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgarian_cuisine) is similar to that of other Balkan countries and demonstrates strong Turkish and Greek influences.[^cite_note-Cuisine-454] [Yogurt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yogurt), [lukanka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lukanka), [banitsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banitsa), [shopska salad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shopska_salad), [lyutenitsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyutenitsa) and [kozunak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kozunak) are among the best-known local foods. Meat consumption is lower than the European average, given a cultural preference for a large variety of salads.[^cite_note-Cuisine-454] Bulgaria was the world's second-largest wine exporter until 1989, but has since lost that position.[^cite_note-455][^cite_note-456] The 2016 harvest yielded 128 million litres of wine, of which 62 million was exported mainly to Romania, Poland and Russia.[^cite_note-457] [Mavrud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mavrud), Rubin, [Shiroka melnishka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siroka_Melniska), [Dimiat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dimiat) and [Cherven Misket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misket_Cherven) are the typical grapes used in [Bulgarian wine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgarian_wine).[^cite_note-458] [Rakia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rakia) is a traditional fruit [brandy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brandy) that was consumed in Bulgaria as early as the 14th century.[^cite_note-459]

### Sports

Main article: [Sport in Bulgaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Bulgaria)

Grigor Dimitrov in 2025

[Grigor Dimitrov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grigor_Dimitrov) at the [2025 Miami Open](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Miami_Open)

Bulgaria appeared at the [first modern Olympic games](48644.md) in 1896, when it was represented by [Swiss](26748.md) [gymnast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gymnastics_at_the_1896_Summer_Olympics) [Charles Champaud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Champaud).[^cite_note-460] Since then, Bulgarian athletes have won 55 gold, 90 silver, and 85 bronze medals,[^cite_note-461] ranking 25th in the [all-time medal table](3729318.md). [Weightlifting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympic_weightlifting) and [wrestling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wrestling) are signature sports of Bulgaria. As of 2025, Bulgaria ranks 2nd in the all-time medal table of the [European Weightlifting Championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Weightlifting_Championships), 3rd in the all-time medal table of the [World Weightlifting Championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Weightlifting_Championships), 4th in the all-time medal table for [Weightlifting at the Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weightlifting_at_the_Summer_Olympics), 3rd in the all-time medal table of the [European Wrestling Championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Wrestling_Championships), 6th in the all-time medal table of the [World Wrestling Championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Wrestling_Championships) and 9th in the all-time medal table for [wrestling at the Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wrestling_at_the_Summer_Olympics). Coach [Ivan Abadzhiev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_Abadjiev) developed innovative training practices that allowed Bulgaria to dominate weightlifting in the 1980s.[^cite_note-Sport-462] Bulgarian athletes have also excelled in [boxing](4243.md), [gymnastics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gymnastics), [volleyball](32558.md) and [tennis](29773.md).[^cite_note-Sport-462] [Stefka Kostadinova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stefka_Kostadinova)'s world record in [women's high jump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_high_jump_world_record_progression), achieved at the [1987 World Championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1987_World_Championships_in_Athletics), remained unbroken until 2024, making it one of the longest-standing world records in history.[^cite_note-463] [Grigor Dimitrov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grigor_Dimitrov) was the first Bulgarian tennis player in the Top 3 [ATP rankings](13480983.md#Current_rankings).[^cite_note-464]

[Football](10568.md) is the most popular sport in the country. The [Bulgarian national football team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgaria_national_football_team)'s best performance was a 4th-place finish at the [1994 FIFA World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_FIFA_World_Cup), when the squad was spearheaded by the top goalscorer for the tournament- [Hristo Stoichkov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hristo_Stoichkov).[^cite_note-Sport-462] Stoichkov is the most successful Bulgarian player of all time; he was awarded the [Golden Boot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Golden_Shoe) and the [Ballon d'Or](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ballon_d'Or) and was considered one of the best players in the world while playing for [FC Barcelona](68187.md) in the 1990s.[^cite_note-465][^cite_note-466] [CSKA Sofia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PFC_CSKA_Sofia) and [Levski Sofia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PFC_Levski_Sofia)[^cite_note-Sport-462] are the most successful clubs domestically and [long-standing rivals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eternal_derby_of_Bulgarian_football).[^cite_note-467] [Ludogorets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PFC_Ludogorets_Razgrad) is remarkable for having advanced from the local fourth division to the [2014–15 UEFA Champions League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014%E2%80%9315_UEFA_Champions_League) group stage in a mere nine years.[^cite_note-468] Placed 39th in 2018, it is Bulgaria's highest-ranked club in [UEFA](32332.md).[^cite_note-469]
